    Headline
    arrhythmia exacerbation, AV block, edema, heart failure, palpitations, QT prolongation, sinus tachycardia
    General Information
    During pre-marketing clinical trials for the treatment of schizophrenia, sinus tachycardia occurred in 3% of patients receiving iloperidone 10 to 16 mg/day (n = 483), 12% of patients receiving iloperidone 20 to 24 mg/day (n = 391), and 1% of those receiving placebo (n = 587). Tachycardia was considered a dose-related adverse reaction in clinical trials. Palpitations were reported in at least 1% of patients in other pre-marketing evaluation. Edema (general, pitting, due to cardiac disease) was reported in 0.1% to 1% of patients receiving iloperidone. Arrhythmia exacerbation, first degree AV block, and acute and congestive heart failure were reported rarely (less than 0.1%). In clinical trials of iloperidone for the treatment of bipolar mania, tachycardia (including sinus tachycardia, postural orthostatic tachycardia syndrome, and heart rate increased) was reported in 23% of patients on iloperidone versus 5% of patients on placebo. Iloperidone has been associated with QT prolongation in some patients. During a phase III trial, the effect of iloperidone on the QT interval was evaluated. The mean change in QTc from baseline was 11.4 milliseconds (msec), and no patient experienced a QT interval above 500 msec. In one open-label study (n = 160), iloperidone 12 mg twice daily was associated with a QTc prolongation of 9 msec. No cases of torsade de pointes were observed during pre-marketing evaluation of the drug.

    Headline
    akathisia, dyskinesia, dystonic reaction, pseudoparkinsonism, torticollis, tremor
    General Information
    During pre-marketing clinical trials in adults with schizophrenia, the incidence of extrapyramidal disorder was similar between iloperidone and placebo groups: 5% of patients receiving iloperidone 10 to 16 mg/day and 4% of patients receiving iloperidone 20 to 24 mg/day vs. 4% with placebo. Tremor occurred in 3% of patients receiving iloperidone 10 to 24 mg/day and 2% of those receiving placebo. The following incidences of specific EPS events were compiled from pooled clinical trial data in patients receiving iloperidone 10 to 16 mg/day, iloperidone 20 to 24 mg/day, or placebo, respectively: akathisia (1.7%, 2.3%, 2.7%), bradykinesia (0.6%, 0.5%, 0%), dyskinesia (1.7%, 1%, 1.5%), dystonic reaction (1%, 0.8%, 0.7%), pseudoparkinsonism (0.2%, 0.3%, 0%), and tremor (2.5%, 3.1%, 1.9%). In other pre-marketing evaluation, torticollis was reported rarely (less than 0.1%). In a placebo-controlled trial of iloperidone for bipolar mania, akathisia was reported in 4.4% of patients receiving iloperidone compared to no reported cases in the placebo group. Other extrapyramidal symptoms, including blepharospasm, dystonia, muscle spasm, restlessness, torticollis, and tremor, were reported at an incidence of 0.5% each in patients receiving iloperidone, with no cases in the placebo arm. The incidence of extrapyramidal symptoms (i.e., akathisia, ataxia or abnormal gait, dystonic reaction, hypokinesia or akinesia, hyperkinesis, hypertonia, hyporeflexia, involuntary muscle contractions, oculogyric crisis, pseudoparkinsonism, tremor) is typically lower with atypical antipsychotics than with conventional antipsychotics such as haloperidol. Dystonic reaction is a potential effect of all antipsychotics, and may occur in susceptible individuals during the first few days of treatment. This effect is observed more commonly in males, younger age groups, and with high potency antipsychotics. Dystonic reactions may manifest as torticollis with or without throat tightness, difficulty swallowing or breathing, oculogyric crisis, trismus, or protrusion of the tongue. Pseudoparkinsonism may occur 1 to 2 weeks after initiation of antipsychotic therapy and is more common in geriatric patients. Akathisia may develop several days to weeks into therapy and may respond to dosage reduction or concomitant administration of a benzodiazepine or a beta-blocker.

    Headline
    tardive dyskinesia
    General Information
    Tardive dyskinesia (TD) is a syndrome of potentially irreversible, involuntary, dyskinetic movements that may develop during treatment with antipsychotic drugs. Involuntary orofacial movements (tongue, mouth, jaw, eyelids, or face) or choreoathetoid movements in the extremities may occur. Factors associated with an increased risk of TD include an age above 55 years, female gender, White or African American, presence of a mood disorder, intellectual disability, CNS injury, prior or current akathisia, significant parkinsonism, or acute dystonic reaction. The rate of TD in adults treated with a first generation antipsychotic appears to be at least 3 times that observed with second generation antipsychotics like iloperidone. It is believed that the likelihood of developing TD increases with prolonged treatment and cumulative doses. Less commonly, it can also develop after short periods of time and with low dosages or may go undetected until the medication is stopped. The use of antipsychotics may mask signs and symptoms of TD; however, TD may also remit partially or completely if the antipsychotic is withdrawn. The effect that symptomatic suppression has upon the long-term course of TD is unknown. In people who require chronic treatment with iloperidone, the lowest possible dose and the shortest duration of treatment should be used. Monitor iloperidone recipients routinely (at 3 to 6 month intervals) for movement disorders using standardized assessments (e.g., AIMS, DISCUS). If signs or symptoms of TD develop, consider discontinuation of iloperidone therapy.

    General Information
    Seizures were reported in 0.1% (1 in 1,344) of iloperidone-treated subjects during pre-marketing clinical trials compared to 0.3% (2 in 587) of those receiving placebo. Because seizures have been reported during the use of other antipsychotics, iloperidone should be used cautiously in people with a history of seizures or a seizure disorder.

Musculoskeletal stiffness was considered a dose-related adverse reaction. Myalgia and muscle spasms (muscle cramps) were reported in at least 1% of patients during pre-marketing evaluation of iloperidone. Elevated creatine kinase, while reported rarely with many atypical antipsychotics, was not reported during clinical studies with iloperidone.

    Headline
    appetite stimulation, fluid retention, hypokalemia, weight gain
    General Information
    Metabolic effects that may occur with use of atypical antipsychotics, including iloperidone, include weight gain. Monitoring of weight is recommended during treatment. Weight gain was considered a dose-related effect during clinical trials. During clinical trials for the treatment of schizophrenia, weight gain occurred in 1% of iloperidone 10 to 16 mg/day recipients and 9% of 20 to 24 mg/day recipients. Pooled data from 4 clinical trials found that a weight gain of at least 7% of body weight occurred in 12% of iloperidone 10 to 16 mg/day recipients and 18% of iloperidone 20 to 24 mg/day recipients vs. 4% with placebo. The mean weight change from baseline was 2 to 2.7 kg in the iloperidone groups. In bipolar mania trials, weight gain (reported as weight increased and BMI increased) occurred in 6% of iloperidone recipients. The mean change in weight was 4.6 kg for iloperidone and 1.6 kg for placebo. The proportion of subjects with weight gain of 7% or more from baseline was 35% for iloperidone (24 mg/day) and 14% for placebo. Metabolic and nutritional effects reported infrequently (0.1% to 1%) with iloperidone therapy include appetite stimulation, dehydration, hypokalemia, and fluid retention.

    Headline
    diabetes mellitus, diabetic ketoacidosis, hyperglycemia
    General Information
    Epidemiological studies suggest an increased risk of hyperglycemia-related adverse effects during use of atypical antipsychotics. These agents, including iloperidone, have been associated with extreme cases of hyperglycemia, diabetic ketoacidosis, hyperosmolar coma, and death. Aggravation of pre-existing diabetes mellitus may occur. Atypical antipsychotics may have effects on glucose metabolism that are independent of their effect on weight gain; although, significant weight gain may contribute to glucose intolerance. One study noted that patients taking atypical agents were 9% more likely to have a new diagnosis of diabetes mellitus than patients taking older therapies. Pooled data from short-term and long-term clinical trials revealed no clinically significant changes in routine chemistry laboratory values, including glucose, from baseline to endpoint in patients receiving iloperidone compared to placebo. The following mean changes in glucose from baseline were reported per length of treatment in patients receiving iloperidone 10 to 16 mg/day versus 20 to 24 mg/day: 3 to 6 months (+1.8 vs. -3.6 mg/dL), 6 to 12 months (+5.4 vs. -9 mg/dL), and greater than 12 months (+5.4 vs. -18 mg/dL). In a 4-week, fixed-dose study of adults with schizophrenia, the mean change in serum glucose from baseline in patients receiving iloperidone 24 mg/day was +6.6 mg/dL versus -0.5 mg/dL with placebo. There was a higher proportion of patients in the active treatment group (10.7%) with a shift in serum glucose from less than 100 mg/dL to at least 126 mg/dL than with placebo (2.5%). In a 4-week fixed dose study of adults with bipolar mania, mean changes from baseline in serum glucose and the proportion of patients with shifts in fasting glucose from normal (less than 100 mg/dL) to high (126 mg/dL or more) were similar for patients on iloperidone vs. placebo treatment. Consider the possibility of impaired glucose tolerance in patients who develop symptoms of hyperglycemia or diabetes, such as polydipsia, polyuria, polyphagia, and weakness. Discontinuation of iloperidone should be considered if symptoms are severe. In some cases, continued antidiabetic treatment has been necessary despite discontinuation of the causative antipsychotic agent.

      GSTerm Name
      hyperglycemia
      Severity
      Moderate
    Headline
    hypercholesterolemia, hyperlipidemia, hypertriglyceridemia
    General Information
    Hyperlipidemia (e.g., hypercholesterolemia, hypertriglyceridemia) has been observed in patients receiving atypical antipsychotics, including iloperidone. In one 4-week, fixed-dose study (24 mg/day) of adults with schizophrenia, the following changes in fasting lipids from baseline occurred in patients receiving iloperidone versus placebo, respectively: total cholesterol (+8.18 mg/dL vs. -2.17 mg/dL), LDL (9.03 mg/dL vs. -1.41 mg/dL), HDL (0.55 mg/dL vs. -3.35 mg/dL), and triglycerides (-0.83 mg/dL vs. 16.47 mg/dL). In the same study, 3.6% of iloperidone-treated patients experienced an increase in total cholesterol from normal to high (from less than 200 mg/dL to 240 mg/dL or more) versus 1.4% of placebo-treated patients; 1.1% of iloperidone-treated patients experienced increases in LDL from normal to high (less than 100 mg/dL to 160 mg/dL or higher) versus 2.4% of placebo-treated patients; 12.1% of iloperidone-treated patients experienced decreases in HDL from normal to low (from 40 mg/dL or more to less than 40 mg/dL) versus 23.8% of placebo-treated patients; and 10.1% of iloperidone-treated patients experienced an increase in triglycerides from normal to high (from less than 150 mg/dL to 200 mg/dL or higher) versus 8.3% of placebo-treated patients. Pooled data from short-term and long-term clinical trials revealed mean decreases in total cholesterol and triglyceride measurements from baseline to endpoint in patients receiving iloperidone 10 to 24 mg/day. In a 4-week fixed dose study of adults with bipolar mania, mean changes from baseline for fasted total cholesterol, LDL, HDL, and triglycerides was similar between iloperidone-treated patients and placebo. The proportion of patients with shifts in fasted total cholesterol from normal to high was greater in the iloperidone-treatment group (10.7%) than with placebo (7.2%). Shifts from normal to high LDL and triglycerides and from normal to low HDL occurred at similar rates between iloperidone-treated patients and placebo.

    Headline
    amenorrhea, galactorrhea, gynecomastia, hyperprolactinemia, impotence (erectile dysfunction), infertility, mastalgia, menorrhagia, menstrual irregularity, osteopenia
    General Information
    Hyperprolactinemia is a response to the dopaminergic antagonism caused by iloperidone. Increased prolactin concentrations are associated with galactorrhea, libido decrease, gynecomastia, menstrual irregularity, amenorrhea, impotence (erectile dysfunction), or reversible infertility in females (i.e., anovulation). Chronic hyperprolactinemia may result in loss of bone density (osteopenia) in both males and females. Amenorrhea and breast pain (mastalgia) were reported in 0.1% to 1% of patients receiving iloperidone for schizophrenia during premarketing evaluation. Impotence (erectile dysfunction) was reported in at least 1% of iloperidone recipients during other pre-marketing evaluation. Galactorrhea occurred in less than 0.3%. Menstrual irregularity, gynecomastia, menorrhagia, metrorrhagia, and postmenopausal hemorrhage were reported rarely (less than 0.1%). During clinical trials in patients with schizophrenia, the mean increase in prolactin concentrations was 2.6 ng/mL in the iloperidone-treated group (24 mg/day) and elevated prolactin levels were observed in 26% of those receiving iloperidone vs. 12% with placebo. Similarly, in a trial of patients with bipolar mania, the mean change from baseline prolactin levels for iloperidone was an increase of 15.66 ng/mL and elevated plasma prolactin levels were observed in 35% of iloperidone recipients. Pooled data from longer term clinical trials involving 3,210 adults indicated that endocrine-related effects were reported more frequently with iloperidone vs. placebo.

    Headline
    infection, stroke
    General Information
    Antipsychotics, including iloperidone, are not approved for the treatment of dementia-related psychosis in geriatric adults. Increased death rates (1.6 to 1.7 times that of placebo) have been noted in this patient population receiving atypical antipsychotics. Death typically occurred due to heart failure, sudden death, or infection (primarily pneumonia). Of 17 placebo controlled trials (n = 5106) performed with olanzapine, aripiprazole, risperidone, or quetiapine in elderly patients with dementia-related psychosis, 15 showed numerical increases in mortality in the active treatment group compared to the placebo-treated patients. A significantly increased incidence of cerebrovascular events (e.g., stroke, transient ischemic attack), including fatalities, have been reported in the elderly with dementia-related psychosis receiving some atypical antipsychotics compared to placebo.

    anaphylactoid reactions, angioedema, pruritus, rash, urticaria
    General Information

Hypersensitivity reactions, including anaphylactoid reactions, angioedema (throat tightness, oropharyngeal swelling, swelling of the face, lips, mouth, and tongue), urticaria, rash, and pruritus have been reported during postmarketing use.

    Headline
    agranulocytosis, anemia, leukopenia, neutropenia
    General Information
    Clinical trial data and postmarketing reports indicate that leukopenia, neutropenia, and agranulocytosis have occurred during the use of antipsychotic agents. Pooled data from four placebo-controlled, short-term studies revealed no clinically significant changes in routine hematology laboratory values from baseline to endpoint in patients receiving iloperidone compared to placebo. However, anemia, iron deficiency anemia, decreased hemoglobin, increased neutrophil count, and decreased hematocrit were reported in 0.1% to 1% of patients during other pre-marketing evaluations of iloperidone for the treatment of schizophrenia. Leukopenia was reported rarely (less than 0.1%). In a placebo-controlled trial of iloperidone for the treatment of bipolar mania, hematocrit below the extended normal range (less than 0.85 x LLN) occurred at least once in 3.5% of patients taking iloperidone versus 0.5% with placebo. Analysis of clinical laboratory data suggest this may be related to hemodilution based on consistent decreases in hematocrit, hemoglobin, white blood cells, total protein, and albumin. These effects on hematocrit and total protein have been observed with other alpha receptor antagonists and are attributed to hemodilution. Patients with a history of drug-induced leukopenia or neutropenia or history of clinically significant low white blood cell (WBC) count should be carefully monitored while receiving an antipsychotic, including regular laboratory monitoring of the complete blood count (CBC) during the first few months of therapy. Consider discontinuing treatment if a clinically significant decline in WBC occurs in the absence of an identifiable cause. Iloperidone should be discontinued in patients who develop severe neutropenia (ANC less than 1,000/mm3).

    General Information

    Headline
    hyponatremia, SIADH, water intoxication
    General Information
    Hyponatremia due to atypical antipsychotics such as iloperidone can develop from polydipsia which can progress to water intoxication, with symptoms such as confusion, lethargy, psychosis, and in severe cases, seizures or death. Some data suggest that antipsychotic-induced hyponatremia is most likely the result of syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone (SIADH).

    Headline
    hyperthermia, hypothermia
    General Information
    Antipsychotic agents can disrupt the hypothalamic mechanism for temperature regulation and increase the risk for hyperthermia. People receiving iloperidone should be advised of conditions that contribute to an elevation in core body temperature (e.g., strenuous exercise, ambient temperature increase, dehydration). Hyperthermia was reported in less than 0.1% of treated subjects during premarketing evaluation of iloperidone. A less frequently described alteration in thermoregulatory processes reported with both conventional and atypical antipsychotics is hypothermia. Thermoregulation is multi-factorial; however, the dopaminergic system appears to have a primary role, and serotonin may also have modulatory activity (5-HT2a receptors). Most cases of hypothermia associated with antipsychotics have occurred in conjunction with other potential precipitating factors such as hypothyroidism, sepsis, organic brain injury, or environmental temperature. Hypothermia appears to occur more frequently during initiation of antipsychotic therapy or after dose increases.

    Headline
    neuroleptic malignant syndrome
    General Information
    A potentially fatal symptom complex referred to as neuroleptic malignant syndrome (NMS) is a potential side effect of all antipsychotics. NMS is characterized by hyperpyrexia, muscle rigidity, alterations in consciousness, altered mental status, and autonomic instability (e.g., irregular pulse or blood pressure, tachycardia, diaphoresis, arrhythmia). Elevated creatine kinase (CK), rhabdomyolysis, and acute renal failure may occur. The cause of NMS is not completely understood; however, dopamine receptor blockade is one of the mechanisms by which NMS is thought to occur. A primary risk factor for developing NMS appears to be the initiation or increase in dose of an antipsychotic. High potency and depot antipsychotics carry the greatest risk. Environmental risk factors include conditions that inhibit heat dissipation such as an elevated ambient room temperature, prolonged heat exposure, the use of individual restraints, or dehydration. NMS occurs more frequently in young adults, which is most likely the result of age of first exposure rather than an age-related risk. NMS occurs more frequently in men, which is thought to be related to the higher likelihood of male vs. female exposure to the causative agent. Risk factors for recurrent NMS include a personal history of NMS, increasing age, and certain medical co-morbidities (e.g., electrolyte imbalances, dehydration). Iloperidone should be immediately discontinued and appropriate supportive therapy initiated as soon as symptoms of NMS are discovered.
    Adverse Reaction GSTerms
      GSTerm Name
      neuroleptic malignant syndrome
      Severity
      Severe

Description
  Text
  Iloperidone is an oral atypical antipsychotic belonging to the benzisoxazole/benzoisothiazol derivatives class. Iloperidone is approved in adult patients for the acute and maintenance treatment of schizophrenia and for the treatment of acute treatment of manic or mixed episodes associated with bipolar I disorder. In a long-term study, iloperidone was effective in delaying time to relapse in patients with schizophrenia who were stabilized on doses ranging from 8 mg/day to 24 mg/day. In a 4-week, placebo-controlled trial of patients with bipolar disorder, iloperidone (24 mg/day) demonstrated superiority over placebo in changes to the Young Mania Rating Scale (YMRS) total score from baseline at Day 28. Both CYP2D6 and CYP3A4 are involved in iloperidone metabolism; therefore, clinically significant drug interactions may occur with CYP2D6 or CYP3A4 inhibitors. In clinical trials, iloperidone was associated with QTc prolongation, an effect that was enhanced during concurrent use of potent CYP2D6 or CYP3A4 inhibitors. Due to the possibility of arrhythmias and sudden death associated with QTc prolongation, consideration should be given to the use of other treatment options prior to iloperidone administration. As with all antipsychotics, iloperidone carries a boxed warning for increased mortality in geriatric patients with dementia-related psychosis. Iloperidone received FDA approval for treatment of schizophrenia in 2009; approval for treatment of bipolar disorder was granted in 2024.

Mechanism Of Action
  Text
  The exact mechanism responsible for the therapeutic effects of iloperidone in the treatment of schizophrenia and bipolar I disorder is unknown. However, it is thought that atypical antipsychotics, including iloperidone, reduce symptoms of schizophrenia and manic or mixed episodes of bipolar disorder through dopamine D2 and serotonin 5-HT2 antagonism. In vitro data indicate that iloperidone has high binding affinity for dopamine D2 and D3 receptors, serotonin 5-HT2A receptors, and norepinephrine NEalpha1 receptors, moderate affinity for D4, 5-HT6, and 5-HT7, and low affinity for 5-HT1A, D1, and histamine (H1) receptors. Iloperidone also exhibits alpha-1 adrenergic receptor antagonism, which has been associated with orthostatic hypotension, dizziness, tachycardia, and syncope. The active metabolites of iloperidone (P88 and P95) are also active, with P88 having an in vitro receptor binding profile similar to the parent drug and P95 showing affinity for 5-HT2A and NEalpha receptors.

Pharmacokinetics
  Route Specific Pharmacokinetics
    Name
    Oral Route
    Description
    Iloperidone is well absorbed with peak plasma concentrations occurring 2 to 4 hours after a dose. Administration with a standard high-fat meal does not have a significant effect on the pharmacokinetics of the drug; therefore, iloperidone may be administered without regard to meals. Steady-state concentrations are reached within 3 to 4 days.
  Special Populations
    Name
    Hepatic Impairment
    Description
    In one study comparing patients with mild hepatic impairment (Child Pugh class A) to healthy controls, no relevant differences in the pharmacokinetics of iloperidone or its metabolites (P88 or P95) were observed. A comparison of patients with moderate hepatic impairment (Child Pugh class B) to healthy controls showed a higher (2-fold) and more variable free exposure to P88 in hepatically impairment patients, whereas exposure to iloperidone and P95 was generally similar between the groups (less than 50% change compared to control). Pharmacokinetic parameters for iloperidone have not been evaluated in patients with severe hepatic impairment (Child Pugh class C).
    Name
    Renal Impairment
    Description
    After single doses, renal impairment (CrCl less than 30 mL/minute) was shown to have minimal effect on the iloperidone Cmax. The AUC of iloperidone was increased by 24%, the AUC of its metabolite (P95) was increased by 52%, and the AUC of its metabolite (P88) was decreased by 6%. Renal impairment alone is unlikely to have a significant effect on the pharmacokinetics of the drug.
    Name
    Other
    Description
    CYP2D6 Poor Metabolizers (PMs)
Due to the changes in pharmacokinetic parameters of iloperidone in CYP2D6 poor metabolizers (PMs) relative to extensive metabolizers, dosage adjustments are required in patients who are CYP2D6 PMs.
 
Smoking
Tobacco smoking does not have an effect on the pharmacokinetics of iloperidone.
  Text
  Iloperidone is administered orally. Iloperidone and its metabolites are about 95% protein bound. The apparent volume of distribution is 1,340 to 2,800 L. Metabolism occurs through carbonyl reduction, hydroxylation (mediated by CYP2D6), and O-demethylation (mediated by CYP3A4). One of the primary metabolites (P95) represents 47.9% of the AUC of iloperidone and its metabolites in plasma for extensive metabolizers (EM) and 25% for poor metabolizers (PM) of CYP2D6. The active metabolite P88 represents 19.5% of total plasma exposure in EMs and 34% in PMs. The mean elimination half-life is 18 hours in EMs and 33 hours in PMs. The mean elimination half-lives of P88 and P95 in EMs are 26 hours and 23 hours, respectively, and in PMs are 37 hours and 31 hours, respectively. Less than 1% of a dose is excreted unchanged. Radioactive studies showed that the majority of radioactive materials were recovered in the urine (mean 58.2% and 45.1% in EMs and PMs, respectively), with 19.9% to 22.1% of the dose recovered in the feces.
 
Affected cytochrome P450 (CYP450) isoenzymes and drug transporters: CYP2D6, CYP3A4
Because iloperidone metabolism occurs via CYP2D6 and CYP3A4, clinically significant drug interactions may occur with strong CYP2D6 inhibitors, strong CYP3A4 inhibitors, and concomitant use of strong CYP2D6 with strong CYP3A4 inhibitors. Iloperidone is an inhibitor of CYP 3A4, 3A5 and 2D6; however, coadministration with sensitive substrates of these enzymes did not appear to result in clinically significant interactions. While iloperidone is a weak P-gp inhibitor, drug interactions of significance do not appear to occur due to P-gp inhibition. Iloperidone and P88 are not P-gp substrates. Iloperidone is not a substrate for CYP 1A1, 1A2, 2A6, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, 2C19, or 2E1 and does not substantially inhibit CYP 1A1, 1A2, 2A6, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, or 2E1. Iloperidone also does not induce CYP 1A2, 2C8, 2C9, 2C19, 3A4 and 3A5.

Dosage Adjustment Guidelines
  Hepatic Impairment
  Mild hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh class A): No dosage adjustments are necessary.
Moderate hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh class B): Caution is recommended due to increased iloperidone exposure; these patients may require dose reduction.
Severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh class C): Use should be avoided.
  Renal Impairment
  Because iloperidone is highly metabolized in the liver, renal impairment is unlikely to have a significant effect on the pharmacokinetics of the drug. It appears that no dosage adjustments are required.
